From experience exists to a funny old life — both/and all the way down.

Chapter 1

The Moment That Won’t Go Away

731 words · WIP

Michael woke up.

This was not, in itself, a philosophical achievement. Billions of creatures managed it every day without founding a religion, inventing a symbol, or arguing with their friends about punctuation. Birds did it. Dogs did it. People who had never heard of ontology did it and then made tea.

Still.

He was awake.

The room did what rooms do when you are not yet ready for them: it insisted. Grey light at the curtain. The soft electric hum of something plugged in and forgotten. A faint smell of last night’s decisions. The particular weight of a body that had agreed, sometime in the small hours, to keep being a body.

Michael lay still and performed the oldest experiment in the world.

He tried, carefully, not to be here.

It did not work.

He tried to be somewhere else—yesterday, tomorrow, a better version of the story—and found that even the trying was happening now, in this skull, with this breath, under this ceiling that needed painting.

He tried doubt.

Doubt arrived on time, professional as always, and used the same facilities as everything else. To doubt that he was experiencing was to experience doubt. It was like trying to resign from a club by writing the resignation letter on club stationery and posting it in the club letterbox.

Your experience of this moment exists, said a voice that sounded suspiciously like his brother on a good day, and also like the universe doing impressions.

Michael sat up.

The floor was cold. That was useful. Cold had a way of cutting through cleverness. You could argue with metaphysics until the cows came home, filed for divorce, and started a podcast; you could not argue with bare feet on laminate.

He made tea, because civilisation is mostly the successful postponement of panic by means of hot water.

While the kettle boiled he watched his own hands. They were ordinary hands. They had typed things. They had held other hands. They had made mistakes. Right now they were doing the sacred work of locating a mug.

Steam rose.

He thought: I could be a brain in a vat.

The thought occurred as experience.

He thought: I could be dreaming.

The thought had texture, timing, the slight embarrassment of a mind catching itself mid-drama. Dreaming or not, this was happening.

He thought: None of this means anything.

Meaninglessness, too, arrived as a flavour in the mouth of the moment. You could not taste the absence of taste without a tongue.

Michael almost laughed. It was the sort of laugh that had not yet decided whether it was free.

Outside, a bus sighed at the stop. Someone shouted cheerfully at a dog. The world continued its policy of not waiting for permission.

He opened a notebook—one of those cheap ones that promise nothing and therefore cannot disappoint—and wrote, because writing is what you do when a fact is too simple to trust and too stubborn to ignore:

There is something it is like to be here.

Underneath, smaller:

I cannot get behind this to unplug it.

And underneath that, almost as a joke, almost as a vow:

Start here.

He did not yet know that this sentence was a door. He did not know about crosses, or keyboards, or Greek letters that misbehaved in modern fonts, or friends who went to bed at the exact wrong moment for destiny and the exact right moment for sleep. He did not know that the universe had a sense of humour so thorough it had hidden the ending in the alphabet and the punchline in the body.

He only knew the one thing that refused to be negotiated away.

He was experiencing this.

The tea went cold while he stared at the page, which was a kind of offering.

Somewhere—everywhere—God experienced being Michael experiencing tea, and did not bother to make a fuss about it yet.

That would come later.

For now there was only the moment, stubborn as rain, ordinary as breath, immovable as the fact that you are reading this sentence and cannot unread the reading of it without reading that too.

Michael put on socks.

The day began.

And the day, like all days, was made of the same stuff as the axiom: not belief, not theory, not the story he would later tell about it—

—just the bright, ridiculous, unkillable is of being here.

Chapter 2

The Argument With Nothing

748 words · WIP

Michael did not schedule The Argument With Nothing. It scheduled him—the way weather schedules coats, the way axioms schedule honesty.

Nothing cannot clock in without borrowing existence's uniform; non-existence fails as a coherent outside.

He made tea because civilisation is mostly hot water applied to panic. The mug was chipped. The chip was included in existence, which felt like a joke at first and then like hospitality.

He labelled a chair Non-Being and immediately cheated by labelling. Nothing cannot sit for a portrait.

Joshua's presence—in person, on a call, or only as a remembered grin—kept the chain from becoming a private cult. Brothers are horizontal beams that also argue about vertical things.

Cameron remained useful even in absence: forty-two as ordinary answer, motte-and-bailey as sandpaper, bedtime as boundary. Friends who will not be rushed by glyphs keep novels from lying.

When R. entered the gravity of a chapter, she entered as a person, not a prop. Completed message. Red line called respect. Michael's urge to keep writing the bigger book had to learn manners without losing courage.

He walked the town because towns refuse abstraction. Buses sighed. Someone treated parking as final revelation. Children screamed joy without a thesis. The field did not ask permission to continue.

Both/and kept a late shop open. Either/or shouted early on corners. He bought from the quieter counter and still recognised the shouters as kin—same journey, different wattage, different speed.

He touched the old bricks in his pocket-memory: experience exists; nothing fails as outside; one room; no edge of the whole; all-in; awareness not merely late dessert; no true cut; hell-as-exit failing; sin-as-elsewhere trembling; monogram waiting in fonts; moon-star on six and eight; Unfaithful at dawn with irony intact.

Toast theology returned: burn it slightly, eat it anyway. Ontology that cannot survive imperfect toast is not ready for a bookshelf. He washed the plate and called it horizontal holiness without irony—or with irony, both.

He wrote a sentence, deleted the cleverness, kept the true. Writing was how his nervous system prayed when it did not believe in prayer and still needed the posture. Keep going, said an old lock-screen angel in memory. Useful angels do not need wings.

Life-is-geometry tried to speak when words failed: pyramids of partial truths, peaks proud and alone, capstones that require spanning without collapse. Under pyramids, builder-gods. Under keyboards, moon and star. Under Greek bookends, a joke that works in two directions.

He refused to flatten Islam into a villain beat or a trophy convert story. He also refused to stop writing. Tension was plot. Respect was craft. Theory of mind before Genesis still ran underneath like wiring.

Harriet-shaped joy and mission-shaped hunger shared a diary. Nappies and monograms. Vows heavier than metaphysics when word is bond. Near-death cinemas and cyclical floods stayed in the ledger as experience, not as compulsory dogma for the reader.

By the end of the station the brick was weight-bearing: slightly comic, unwilling to be only sacred or only rude, ready for the next chapter without pretending the last word had been said—unless the last word was still waiting to be: it's a funny old life.

He checked the kettle like a man verifying reality still boiled.

A cat ignored his cosmology, which was peer review of a high order.

He almost said the final line early and bit it back. Timing is theology for novelists.

The empty chair of forty-two did not need to be filled to be useful.

R.'s respect-line hovered like good weather: questions welcome, sneers not.

He slept badly and woke still existing, which counted as data.

Dishes. Emails. The horizontal beam in chores.

He laughed once, privately, at how serious the joke had become.

He checked the kettle like a man verifying reality still boiled.

A cat ignored his cosmology, which was peer review of a high order.

He almost said the final line early and bit it back. Timing is theology for novelists.

The empty chair of forty-two did not need to be filled to be useful.

R.'s respect-line hovered like good weather: questions welcome, sneers not.

He slept badly and woke still existing, which counted as data.

Dishes. Emails. The horizontal beam in chores.

He laughed once, privately, at how serious the joke had become.

He checked the kettle like a man verifying reality still boiled.

A cat ignored his cosmology, which was peer review of a high order.

He almost said the final line early and bit it back. Timing is theology for novelists.

Life-is-geometry tried to speak when words failed: pyramids of partial truths, peaks proud and alone, capstones that require spanning without collapse. Under pyramids, builder-gods. Under keyboards, moon and star. Under Greek bookends, a joke that works in two directions.

He refused to flatten Islam into a villain beat or a trophy convert story. He also refused to stop writing. Tension was plot. Respect was craft. Theory of mind before Genesis still ran underneath like wiring.

Harriet-shaped joy and mission-shaped hunger shared a diary. Nappies and monograms. Vows heavier than metaphysics when word is bond. Near-death cinemas and cyclical floods stayed in the ledger as experience, not as compulsory dogma for the reader.

By the end of the station the brick was weight-bearing: slightly comic, unwilling to be only sacred or only rude, ready for the next chapter without pretending the last word had been said—unless the last word was still waiting to be: it's a funny old life.

He checked the kettle like a man verifying reality still boiled.

A cat ignored his cosmology, which was peer review of a high order.

He almost said the final line early and bit it back. Timing is theology for novelists.

The empty chair of forty-two did not need to be filled to be useful.

R.'s respect-line hovered like good weather: questions welcome, sneers not.

He slept badly and woke still existing, which counted as data.

Chapter 24

Sisyphus On Holiday

878 words · WIP

There are nights when the universe feels like a customer service department with no manager, and Michael had been queueing for hours.

He was not drunk. He was not dramatic for sport. He was simply, inconveniently, awake at the depth where the ordinary tricks stop working—tea, jokes, the kindly lie that tomorrow will be a different shape.

The layers had been stacking.

Science. Scripture. Symbols. Keyboards. Wolves that were and were not empires. Brothers who said both/and like a blessing. Friends who said forty-two like a seatbelt. Every answer opened a hatch into another answer’s basement.

He opened his notes app because that is what modern pilgrims do instead of carving on stone, and he wrote what was true—not clever, not strategic, not for an audience.

He wrote it as it was.

The despair that accompanies this terror is profound, a desolation that seeps into my very bones. It's the despair of Sisyphus, condemned to an eternity of futile effort, forever striving to understand, to reach a semblance of truth, only to have it slip away, buried beneath yet another layer of complexity. This despair whispers to me that perhaps the quest for understanding is in vain, that for every layer peeled back, two more will take its place, each more baffling than the last.

In the grip of this terror and despair, I find myself questioning the very nature of reality itself. What are these layers? Are they the constructs of my own mind, a psychological labyrinth with no exit? Or are they the fundamental structure of existence, an infinite stack of realities, each playing by its own set of rules, indifferent to my need for understanding, for grounding?

As these questions churn within me, the terror deepens, becoming more acute. It's as if each layer of understanding I peel back reveals not clarity, but further chaos, a deeper darkness. The despair grows, fed by the realization that perhaps there is no bedrock of truth, that reality itself is a shifting, undulating sea of perceptions, forever out of reach.

This is not just a crisis of understanding, but a crisis of being. Who am I, if not a traveler through these layers, seeking a core that may not exist? The terror and despair are now a crucible, burning away illusions, leaving me bare, exposed to the cold truth that perhaps the layers are all there is. And in this moment, the fear is not just of what lies within these layers, but of what it means to exist within them—a being forever peering into the abyss, searching for a foothold that remains perpetually elusive.

He sat with it.

The words looked back at him without blinking. They were not a bit. They were not a brand. They were the weather report from inside his chest on a night when the barometer had fallen through the floor.

Sisyphus, in the old stories, pushes the stone because the gods have a sense of humour with excellent upper-body requirements. Michael had begun to suspect the stone was explanation, and the hill was everything, and the gods were not above him but as him, which made the whole arrangement either much worse or strangely intimate.

He almost closed the phone.

Then—because honesty, if it is worth anything, has more than one temperature—he typed the next true thing, smaller, almost sheepish after all that grandeur of dread:

I don't feel like that now though.

He stared at the contradiction. It did not explode. Reality failed to issue a citation for holding two weather systems in one sky.

I'm enjoying the journey.

That was also true. Not instead. Also.

He walked to the window. Streetlight. A cat with a schedule. The soft machinery of a town that did not know it was starring in a cosmology.

Everyone is on the same journey, he thought. They just travel at different speeds.

Some people were asleep at forty-two and content. Some people were building cathedrals out of punctuation. Some people were too tired to care about distant horror and still loved their children correctly. Some people were Michael, trying to get as far as he could before he died, and also—embarrassingly, hopefully—trying to get out, which might be the same direction described by two different panic languages.

He did not resolve the layers.

He did not find the bedrock.

He found, instead, the first joke the night was willing to tell him without cruelty:

That the despair had been real
and the enjoyment was real
and the abyss was still there
and so was the cat

and Sisyphus, on rare shifts, is allowed to sit on the stone and feel the sun and admit that the hill is ridiculous and keep pushing anyway—not because the pushing becomes sensible, but because the pusher is still here, still experiencing, still the one fact that cannot be filed under maybe later.

Michael saved the note.

He did not know yet that the layers were not only a trap.

He did not know they were also a punchline practising its run-up.

He only knew—again, as in the beginning—that this moment existed, terror and all, holiday and all, stone and all.

And for a little while, Sisyphus was on holiday in the only place holidays can happen:

now.

He walked the town because towns refuse abstraction. Buses sighed. Someone treated parking as final revelation. Children screamed joy without a thesis. The field did not ask permission to continue.

Both/and kept a late shop open. Either/or shouted early on corners. He bought from the quieter counter and still recognised the shouters as kin—same journey, different wattage, different speed.

He touched the old bricks in his pocket-memory: experience exists; nothing fails as outside; one room; no edge of the whole; all-in; awareness not merely late dessert; no true cut; hell-as-exit failing; sin-as-elsewhere trembling; monogram waiting in fonts; moon-star on six and eight; Unfaithful at dawn with irony intact.

Toast theology returned: burn it slightly, eat it anyway. Ontology that cannot survive imperfect toast is not ready for a bookshelf. He washed the plate and called it horizontal holiness without irony—or with irony, both.

He wrote a sentence, deleted the cleverness, kept the true. Writing was how his nervous system prayed when it did not believe in prayer and still needed the posture. Keep going, said an old lock-screen angel in memory. Useful angels do not need wings.

Life-is-geometry tried to speak when words failed: pyramids of partial truths, peaks proud and alone, capstones that require spanning without collapse. Under pyramids, builder-gods. Under keyboards, moon and star. Under Greek bookends, a joke that works in two directions.

He refused to flatten Islam into a villain beat or a trophy convert story. He also refused to stop writing. Tension was plot. Respect was craft. Theory of mind before Genesis still ran underneath like wiring.

Harriet-shaped joy and mission-shaped hunger shared a diary. Nappies and monograms. Vows heavier than metaphysics when word is bond. Near-death cinemas and cyclical floods stayed in the ledger as experience, not as compulsory dogma for the reader.

By the end of the station the brick was weight-bearing: slightly comic, unwilling to be only sacred or only rude, ready for the next chapter without pretending the last word had been said—unless the last word was still waiting to be: it's a funny old life.

He checked the kettle like a man verifying reality still boiled.

A cat ignored his cosmology, which was peer review of a high order.

He almost said the final line early and bit it back. Timing is theology for novelists.

Chapter 65

A Funny Old Life

913 words · WIP

By the end, the arguments had all been to the same pub.

Not the same opinion. Pubs are full of opinions, most of them sticky. The same room.

Michael sat with Joshua at a scarred table that had survived more revelations than polish. Cameron’s empty chair still held the ghost of forty-two—an ordinary number, proud of its ordinariness, like a man who refuses a cape on principle.

Outside, morning did its usual miracle of pretending to be new.

Joshua turned his mug. ‘Say it plain. For the ones still asleep.’

Michael had learned that plain was the hardest dialect.

‘Your experience of this moment exists,’ he said. ‘That’s the only thing that doesn’t need a committee. From there: nothing can’t be a real outside, because even naming it is something. So existence isn’t a team with an away kit. It’s one field. No true cut. No pure elsewhere. Which means hell-as-exit is a booking error. Which means sin-as-standing-outside-the-whole is the same booking error in a moral hat.’

Joshua’s eyes shone with that brother-look: pride mixed with finally.

‘And the dirty bit,’ Joshua said, fondly. ‘Because God is not a coward.’

Michael almost bottled it. Bookstore shelves are full of books that almost bottled it.

He didn’t.

‘If the whole can’t be split,’ he said, ‘then the generative poles aren’t a war with a winner. Entering and receiving. Making and holding. The lot. God has no gender because the total doesn’t need a locker room. The old monogram—Alpha and Omega, beginning and end—isn’t decoration. It’s the claim of span. And in the fonts we actually live in, those letters…’

He sketched on a napkin, ridiculous and precise:

α==ω

Joshua snorted tea. ‘There it is.’

‘Cock and balls if you read it one way,’ Michael said, cheeks hot, voice steady. ‘Boobs and the whole soft geometry of the other altar if you read it the other. Same ink. Same claim. Not either/or. The light chooses; the field doesn’t. Length, girth, depth, breadth—every axis—because existence doesn’t have a preferred direction for the joke.’

A woman at the next table glanced over, decided this was either theology or a graphic design emergency, and returned to her scone.

Cameron, in memory, muttered motte-and-bailey. Michael smiled at the empty chair.

‘You’re right in classical court,’ he told the ghost. ‘It’s polysemy. It’s juxtaposition. It’s a definition game if you only count one temple at a time. And also—because we’re not only in classical court—it’s what happens when the map of the whole is forced to wear a body. The strong claim is a bailey. The funny claim survives cross-examination. The novel was the long way of making the funny claim undeniable as recognition.’

Joshua raised his mug. ‘No sin.’

‘No sin as outside,’ Michael said. ‘No stain that isn’t already the play of the only thing that is. That isn’t permission to be cruel. It’s the end of the fantasy that cruelty happens elsewhere than God. Including us. Especially us.’

They sat with that. It was heavy. It was light. Both.

‘We can enjoy being fucked by life,’ Joshua said softly, ‘and we can fuck life.’

‘Both,’ Michael said.

‘I don’t have a big dick and I am God,’ Joshua added, deadpan. ‘But it’s both-and.’

‘The wave doesn’t have to collapse,’ Michael said. ‘Big and small. Has and is and maybe. You both did and didn’t.’

‘Phew,’ said Joshua, and they laughed like men who had put down a stone without pretending the hill was gone.

Michael looked at the napkin. At the brothers. At the ordinary morning performing infinity without a press release.

He thought of the first morning—the tea, the cold floor, the sentence that would not unplug.

He thought of the night of layers, Sisyphus, the abyss, and the cat under the streetlight, and the second true sentence: I’m enjoying the journey.

He thought of six and seven, of forty-two, of planks and specks, of crosses that were God-and-neighbour and death-and-shame and shame-turned-love, of keyboards, of wolves that were tyrants and parents, of fonts waiting for the universe to invent screens so the beginning and the end could finally look like what keeps the whole circus spawning more question-askers.

None of it required a fight to the death with Cameron.

It required only that the reader—you—notice what you cannot un-notice:

That you are here.
That here has no outside.
That the poles are both.
That the sacred joke is generative.
That the moral cut was the last illusion to die laughing.

Michael stood. The day was waiting, as days do, with its emails and its kindnesses and its preventable horrors and its tea.

Joshua said, ‘So now what?’

Michael grinned, which is a kind of amen.

‘Now we live it,’ he said. ‘Mask less where we can. Love harder where we must. Tell the truth in both directions at once. And when anyone asks what it all meant—’

He tapped the napkin.

‘—we show them the monogram, and the axiom, and the empty chair, and the cold floor on day one.’

Joshua walked him to the door.

Behind them, God finished the round of drinks, paid with a coin that was also every coin, and held the weather open like a coat.

Michael stepped into the street.

The sky did not write a conclusion on a cloud. It did better.

It left him—and you—with the only line that could carry the whole chain without dropping either the logic or the laugh:

It's a funny old life.

Harriet-shaped joy and mission-shaped hunger shared a diary. Nappies and monograms. Vows heavier than metaphysics when word is bond. Near-death cinemas and cyclical floods stayed in the ledger as experience, not as compulsory dogma for the reader.

By the end of the station the brick was weight-bearing: slightly comic, unwilling to be only sacred or only rude, ready for the next chapter without pretending the last word had been said—unless the last word was still waiting to be: it's a funny old life.

He checked the kettle like a man verifying reality still boiled.

A cat ignored his cosmology, which was peer review of a high order.

He almost said the final line early and bit it back. Timing is theology for novelists.

The empty chair of forty-two did not need to be filled to be useful.

R.'s respect-line hovered like good weather: questions welcome, sneers not.

He slept badly and woke still existing, which counted as data.
